## <span id="page-3-0"></span>**Executive summary**

This report provides an overview of key findings and the current status of coral reefs surveyed by the NOAA Pacific Reef Assessment and Monitoring Program (RAMP) in the National Marine Sanctuary of American Samoa (NMSAS) and surrounding territorial waters between June and July 2018, as well as a temporal comparison to similar surveys conducted during the 2015 RAMP research cruise.

--- SENTENCE 10 ---
These surveys are a component of the Pacific Reef Assessment and Monitoring Program (Pacific RAMP)—a long-term, integrated, ecosystem-scale study led by NOAA's Pacific Islands Fisheries Science Center (PIFSC), Ecosystem Sciences Division (ESD), at about 40 U.S.-affiliated Pacific islands.

--- SENTENCE 11 ---
The report is divided into three thematic sections:

1- Benthic composition and coral demographics:

--- SENTENCE 12 ---
This section reports on site- and sub-island/atoll level (hereafter referred to as "reporting units"), estimates of (1) benthic cover for hard corals, crustose coralline algae, macroalgae, and turf algae; (2) colony density (no. colonies m<sup>−</sup><sup>2</sup> ) of juvenile (<5 cm) and adult (≥5 cm) corals; (3) partial mortality as percentage of old and recent dead; (4) prevalence of bleaching (all levels of severity combined: pale to white), and acute and chronic diseases; and (5) coral generic richness.

--- SENTENCE 13 ---
#### 2- Reef fishes:

--- SENTENCE 14 ---
This section reports on the site- and sub-island/atoll level estimates of (1) biomass density (g m<sup>−</sup><sup>2</sup> ) of all fishes, piscivores, fishes >50 cm total length (TL), and the IUCN listed *Acanthurus lineatus* and (2) generic and species richness of the fish assemblages.

--- SENTENCE 15 ---
3- Ocean and climate change:

--- SENTENCE 16 ---
This section reports on (1) the time series of representative subsurface temperature (°C) recorders and (2) site- and island/atoll-level estimates of reef aragonite saturation state and other seawater carbonate chemistry parameters.

--- SENTENCE 17 ---
For the purpose of this report, biological variables were summarized at differing geographic reporting units based on the sub-island/atoll level according to their management status as follows.

--- SENTENCE 18 ---
Sanctuary Management Areas include: Swains Island/Swains Sanctuary, Rose Atoll Sanctuary, Ta'u Sanctuary, Aunu'u zones A and B, and Fagatele/Fagalua (which includes Fagatele bay and Fogama'a/Fagalua bay).

--- SENTENCE 19 ---
Non-Sanctuary Areas include: Ofu and Olosega islands, Ta'u, and Tutuila NE, NW, SE, and SW.

--- SENTENCE 20 ---
In 2018, NOAA Ship delays shifted survey execution by 4 months from February–March (when they traditionally occur) to June–July.

--- SENTENCE 21 ---
Unfortunately, high winds and rough sea conditions in June and July limited small boats' access to much of the east- and south-facing forereef habitats, thereby reducing the spatial extent of the 2018 surveys in comparison to 2015.

--- SENTENCE 22 ---
With the exception of the Ta'u Sanctuary and the Tutuila NW sector, the number of sites surveyed in 2018 for all other reporting units was on average <50% the effort undertaken in 2015.

--- SENTENCE 23 ---
As a result, the graphical output comparing the 2018 and 2015 survey data needs to be interpreted with caution.

--- SENTENCE 24 ---
Salient findings of this study include:

● At the regional level, there was a significant decline in coral cover from 28.7% ± 2.6 in 2015 to 18.2% ± 2.0 in 2018, a 30% increase in crustose coralline algae (CCA) cover from 17.0% ± 1.6 in 2015 to 22.2% ± 2.5 in 2018, and a substantial reduction of macroalgae from 3.4% ± 0.6 in 2015 to 0.7% ± 0.3 in 2018.

-

--- SENTENCE 25 ---
For all reporting units, density of adult coral colonies in 2018 was greater than that of juveniles across depth strata.
- Mean coral generic richness decreased for most reporting units between 2015 and 2018; however, those differences were not significant except for NE Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 26 ---
Despite the loss in mean richness, total generic richness per reporting unit did not change over time.

--- SENTENCE 27 ---
- Coralline orange lethal disease (CLOD) was patchily distributed, and occurrence varied extensively between survey years, as well as within and between reporting units.

--- SENTENCE 28 ---
These differences were driven by reduced number of sites surveyed in 2018 (e.g., Fagatele/Fagalua Sanctuary area) and/or the absence of cases observed in either survey year (e.g., NE Tutuila).

--- SENTENCE 29 ---
Contrastingly, no lesions were recorded at Rose Atoll and Swains Island.
- Two sites along both the northern forereef of Rose and Swains had notably large total fish biomass driven by the biomass of large piscivorous fishes (>50 cm TL).

--- SENTENCE 30 ---
Piscivorous fishes were present at most of the surveyed sites in American Samoa, with the exception of Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 31 ---
Large fishes were not observed in Fagatele or Fagalua.
- *Acanthurus lineatus* species was notably rare at Swains.
- Total fish biomass significantly increased between 2015 and 2018 at Fagatele and Rose Sanctuary management areas, as well as Ta'u and SE Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 32 ---
Substantial increases in the biomass of *Acanthurus lineatus* between 2015 and 2018 were recorded at NW Tutuila, SW Tutuila, and Ofu and Olosega.

--- SENTENCE 33 ---
However, observed biomass was also highly variable at those reporting units.

--- SENTENCE 34 ---
- Omega aragonite values in American Samoa are traditionally the highest measured across ~40 islands and atolls periodically visited by NOAA's Pacific RAMP.

--- SENTENCE 35 ---
Locally, Rose Atoll typically exhibits the highest omega aragonite levels and Tutuila the lowest.

--- SENTENCE 36 ---
Within the islands of American Samoa there is not a lot of temporal variation, hence Omega aragonite values were very similar between survey years 2015 and 2018.
-

--- SENTENCE 37 ---
In 2015–2017, summer sea surface temperatures exceeded the bleaching threshold for several months during each year.

--- SENTENCE 38 ---
These sharp and repeated ocean temperature rises during the 2015–2016 El Niño caused prolonged thermal stress and subsequent coral bleaching, which was recorded by the coral demographic surveys in March–April 2015.

--- SENTENCE 39 ---
During this warming event, Swains Island experienced the greatest thermal anomalies with water temperatures of almost 31°C and consequently, the most extensive levels of coral bleaching.

--- SENTENCE 40 ---
By the summer of 2018, the ocean warming event had receded, and temperatures were within the typical range.

--- SENTENCE 41 ---
## <span id="page-5-0"></span>**Methods**

In 2018, biological surveys (benthic and fish) implemented a modified one-stage stratified random sampling (StRS) design (Ault et al. 2006; Smith et al. 2011) to assess the survey domain which encompassed all hard-bottom reef habitat from 0 to 30 m deep within the islands of American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 42 ---
The stratification scheme incorporated three depth categories—i.e., shallow (0–6 m), mid-depth (>6–18 m), and deep (>18–30 m) where present, and a number of geographic reporting units with differing management status—i.e., Sanctuary Management Areas: Swains Island Sanctuary, Rose Atoll, Ta'u Sanctuary, Aunu'u zones A and B, and Fagatele/Fagalua; non-Sanctuary Areas: Swains Island (only reef fishes), Ofu and Olosega islands, Ta'u, and Tutuila NE, NW, SE, and SW.

--- SENTENCE 43 ---
A complete list of reporting units for benthic and fish surveys is available in Table 1 and Table 3.

--- SENTENCE 44 ---
Rapid Ecological Assessment (REA) sites were randomly selected within each stratum (depth/reporting unit combination), with survey effort proportional to the hard-bottom area within each of the specified strata.

--- SENTENCE 45 ---
In 2018, the delay in NOAA ship operations shifted sampling to the peak of the high wind season (June– July) resulting in a substantial decrease in the number of surveys, particularly throughout east- and southfacing coral reef habitats.

--- SENTENCE 46 ---
With the exception of the Ta'u Sanctuary and the Tutuila NW, the number of StRS sites surveyed in 2018 for all other reporting units was on average <50% the effort undertaken in 2015.

--- SENTENCE 47 ---
As such, reporting in many Sanctuary Management Areas, with the exception of Rose, is limited due to low sample size.

--- SENTENCE 48 ---
This was especially true for the Aunu'u Research Zone (zone B) where adverse conditions completely prevented any surveys.

--- SENTENCE 49 ---
As a result, the graphical output below on the differences between the 2018 and 2015 survey years need to be interpreted with caution.

### <span id="page-5-1"></span>**Benthic Composition and Coral Demographics**

In 2018, benthic and coral community structure and composition surveys at each site were conducted along one, 30-m belt transect.

--- SENTENCE 50 ---
Adult coral colonies (≥5 cm) were surveyed, bottom-time permitting, in two to four, 1.0 × 2.5-m segments, located at the 0–2.5 m, 5.0–7.5 m, 10–12.5 m, and 15–17.5 m mark along the transect.

--- SENTENCE 51 ---
Survey area covered a total of 5 to 10 m2 per transect.

--- SENTENCE 52 ---
All colonies whose center fell within 0.5 m on either side of the transect line were identified to the genus level (Appendix 1), measured for size (maximum diameter to nearest cm), and morphologically classified.

--- SENTENCE 53 ---
In addition, partial mortality and condition of each colony were assessed.

--- SENTENCE 54 ---
Partial mortality was estimated as percent of the colony in terms of 'old dead' and 'recent dead,' and causation of recent mortality was ascribed only when evident.

--- SENTENCE 55 ---
The condition of each colony, including disease and bleaching, was noted, along with the extent (percent of colony affected) and severity (index scaled from mild to acute).

--- SENTENCE 56 ---
Juvenile coral colonies (<5 cm) were surveyed along three 1.0 m wide by 1.0 long segments at the 0–1.0 m, 5.0–6.0 m, and 10.0–11.0 m (covering 3 m2 ) mark of the transect.

--- SENTENCE 57 ---
Juvenile colonies were distinguished in the field by a distinct tissue and skeletal boundary (not a fragment of a larger colony).

--- SENTENCE 58 ---
Each juvenile colony was identified to genus and measured for size by recording both the maximum and perpendicular diameter to the nearest 5 mm.

--- SENTENCE 59 ---
Finally, crustose coralline algae lethal orange disease (CLOD) was enumerated and recorded.

--- SENTENCE 60 ---
Methodological details can be found in Swanson et al. (2018).

--- SENTENCE 61 ---
Still photographs were collected using the photoquadrat method to document the benthic community composition at predetermined points along the transect line with a high-resolution digital camera mounted on a pole.

--- SENTENCE 62 ---
The camera was white balanced with an 18% grey card prior to taking imagery.

--- SENTENCE 63 ---
Photographs were taken every meter from the 1-m to the 30-m mark.

--- SENTENCE 64 ---
Benthic imagery was collected at additional stratified random sites surveyed for fish assemblages, using the same approach.

--- SENTENCE 65 ---
This generated 30 photographs per benthic and fish survey site, all of which were analyzed implementing the computer software CoralNet (Beijbon et al. 2015).

--- SENTENCE 66 ---
Recently, Williams et al. (2019) established that a fully automated CoralNet 'robot,' trained on previous imagery, generated site-level coral cover and CCA cover comparable to human analysts.

--- SENTENCE 67 ---
Therefore, to efficiently extract cover data from 2018 surveys, we used the fully automated CoralNet robot.

--- SENTENCE 68 ---
Benthic taxa were analyzed to the functional group level, including hard corals, coralline algae, macroalgae, and turf algae While we provide robot-generated macro and turf algae cover, the robot's ability to accurately identify these categories is less accurate than human analysts.

--- SENTENCE 69 ---
A list of benthic metrics surveyed and reported herein is presented in Table 2.

--- SENTENCE 70 ---
Benthic cover estimates were generated from photoquadrat surveys conducted for benthic and fish assemblages and, therefore, included larger sample sizes for both Sanctuary and non-Sanctuary management areas.

--- SENTENCE 71 ---
Coral demographic estimates were generated from surveys only conducted for benthic assemblages, the unbalanced sample sizes between survey years precludes some broad statements pertaining to spatial and temporal patterns and trends.

--- SENTENCE 72 ---
For each reporting unit [\(Table 1;](#page-6-0)

--- SENTENCE 73 ---
[Figure 1\)](#page-7-1), sites within each depth stratum were averaged and weighted proportional to stratum area.

--- SENTENCE 74 ---
Weighted averages were then summed across depth strata to obtain a mean estimate for each reporting unit.

--- SENTENCE 75 ---
If there were not at least two sites in a reporting unit per each of the three depth strata within a survey year, then that reporting unit-year mean was removed from the summary analyses presented herein.

--- SENTENCE 76 ---
The mean weighted estimates for each reporting unit [\(Table 1\)](#page-6-0) were then used for the spatial and temporal comparisons.

--- SENTENCE 77 ---
Comparative statistics between the survey years 2015 (NOAA 2016) and 2018 (this study) were computed only for those metrics with appropriate sample sizes, based on the 95% confidence interval of the difference in means, whereby if the 95% confidence interval of the difference between means includes zero, the difference between the means is not significant (P > 0.05).

Coralline lethal orange disease occurrence index (DOI) was estimated as the number of cases (counts) relative to the percent of CCA cover at each survey site as follows: DOI = [(total # of CLOD cases) ÷

--- SENTENCE 89 ---
For each reporting unit, CLOD occurrence estimates were computed by directly averaging all sites with each reporting unit; no proportional weighting by depth stratum area was implemented.

--- SENTENCE 90 ---
Values close to or greater than 1 suggest high disease occurrence; comparatively, numbers close to zero indicate low occurrence.

--- SENTENCE 91 ---
No temporal comparative statistics were computed for CLOD occurrence due the unbalanced sample size between survey years.

Sampling area was<br>standardized across all sites and years.

--- SENTENCE 101 ---
|
| Benthic Cover                     | Percentage of substrate covered by a given benthic category (e.g.,<br>coral, CCA, macroalgae, turf algae)       |
| CLOD occurrence                   | Number of coralline lethal orange disease cases ÷ CCA cover                                                     |

### <span id="page-7-0"></span>**Reef Fishes**

The survey protocol followed the modified stationary point count (SPC) method developed by Ault et al. (2006).

--- SENTENCE 102 ---
Upon reaching a target survey site, a 30-m transect line was laid across the substrate following the bottom contour.

--- SENTENCE 103 ---
Divers used the transect line to locate the centers (7.5 m and 22.5 m) and two edges (0 m and 15 m; or 15 m and 30 m) of their visually estimated 15-m cylindrical survey plot.

--- SENTENCE 104 ---
Each SPC consisted of two components: a 5-minute species enumeration period in which divers recorded all species present in or moving through their cylinder, followed by a tallying portion, in which divers systematically recorded the number and size (total length to nearest cm) of all fishes of each taxon on their list.

--- SENTENCE 105 ---
The tallying

portion was conducted as a series of rapid visual sweeps, with one species grouping counted per sweep.

--- SENTENCE 106 ---
The goal was to obtain a near-instantaneous record of fishes present within each cylinder.

--- SENTENCE 107 ---
In cases where a species was observed during the enumeration period but not present in the cylinder during the tallying period, divers recorded their best estimates of the size and number observed in their first encounter during the enumeration period and marked the data recorded as "non-instantaneous".

--- SENTENCE 108 ---
Herein, both instantaneous and non-instantaneous data are pooled together.

--- SENTENCE 109 ---
Details of our specific survey methods can be found in Ayotte et al. (2015).

--- SENTENCE 110 ---
Finally, 30 digital still photographs were collected along the 30-m transect to record the benthic community composition.

--- SENTENCE 111 ---
Images were analyzed using the software CoralNet following the benthic protocol described above.

--- SENTENCE 112 ---
For all reef fish surveys, data from the two adjacent cylinders were pooled into a mean value for each site.

--- SENTENCE 113 ---
Using the count and size estimate data collected in each replicate survey, the body-weight of each individual fish was calculated using length-to-weight (LW) conversion parameters and, where necessary, length-length (LL) parameters.

--- SENTENCE 114 ---
LW and LL conversion parameters were taken from FishBase (Froese and Pauly 2010; Kulbicki et al. 2005).

--- SENTENCE 115 ---
Biomass per fish was calculated using the standard length-weight equation.

--- SENTENCE 116 ---
For each reporting unit [\(Table 3\)](#page-8-0), sites within each stratum were averaged and weighted proportional to stratum area.

--- SENTENCE 117 ---
Weighted averages were then summed across strata to obtain biomass density estimates for each reporting unit.

--- SENTENCE 118 ---
If a reporting unit did not have at least two sites per depth strata —i.e., shallow (0–6 m), mid-depth (>6–18 m), and deep (>18–30 m)—in a survey year, then that reporting unit-year mean was removed from the summary analyses presented herein.

--- SENTENCE 119 ---
The mean weighted estimates for each reporting unit [\(Table 3\)](#page-8-0) were then used for the spatial and temporal comparisons.

--- SENTENCE 120 ---
Comparative statistics between the survey years 2015 (NOAA 2016) and 2018 (this study) were computed based on the 95% confidence interval of the difference in means, following the same approach as for the benthic metrics, for those for those reef fish metrics with appropriate sample sizes.

### <span id="page-9-0"></span>**Ocean and Climate Change**

Ocean and climate change monitoring surveys were achieved by means of *in situ* observations of ocean temperature; autonomous and discrete grab sampling for analyses of near reef and surface seawater carbonate chemistry; and distinct biological installations designed to provide integrated, ecosystem-wide response data (e.g., calcification).

--- SENTENCE 131 ---
Methodological details can be found in the 2018 Pacific RAMP Project Report (NMFS 2018).

--- SENTENCE 132 ---
Temperature data were collected using high-accuracy temperature loggers (Sea-Bird Scientific®, models: SBE39 and SBE56).

--- SENTENCE 133 ---
For reporting, temperature sensor deployments were grouped by site, and temperature data from successive deployments at each site were concatenated.

--- SENTENCE 134 ---
Raw data were averaged hourly, and gaps of longer than one hour in the time-series (due to instrument failure or battery death) were padded with null values.

--- SENTENCE 135 ---
Aragonite saturation state (Omega aragonite) data were estimated based on discrete seawater samples analyzed for two parameters: dissolved inorganic carbon (DIC) and total alkalinity (TA).

--- SENTENCE 136 ---
Because the marine carbonate system is influenced by salinity, temperature, and pressure, these data were obtained from CTD casts and used during analysis with DIC and TA values to calculate values for omega aragonite.

--- SENTENCE 137 ---
All carbonate system collection and measurement methodologies followed Dickinson et al.

--- SENTENCE 138 ---
Raw site-level water chemistry data, salinity, temperature, and pressure derived from CTD casts are presented in Appendix 5.

## <span id="page-10-0"></span>**Results**

## <span id="page-10-1"></span>**Benthic Composition and Coral Demographics**

#### <span id="page-10-2"></span>2018 Site-level Data

The site-level bubble maps highlight the spatially variable nature of the benthic cover data across islands and management areas

--- SENTENCE 139 ---
Site-level benthic cover of principal benthic functional groups: hard corals, crustose coralline algae (CCA), macroalgae, and turf algae for reporting units in American Samoa surveyed in 2018.

--- SENTENCE 140 ---
In 2018, coral cover was variable and moderate within and between the reporting units, with Fagalua and Rose Atoll exhibiting comparatively lower coral cover compared to other areas.

--- SENTENCE 141 ---
CCA cover was moderately high across the reporting units, with Rose Atoll, as expected from historical records, ranking higher than the rest.

--- SENTENCE 142 ---
Contrastingly, fleshy macroalgae cover was notably low across the survey areas, albeit found at a handful of sites around Swains Island.

--- SENTENCE 143 ---
Turf algae showed variable but moderately high levels, particularly at Tutuila, Ofu and Olosega and Ta'u, with the lower levels of turf algae recorded at both Rose Atoll and Swains Island.

!

--- SENTENCE 144 ---
[](_page_11_Figure_1.jpeg)

<span id="page-11-0"></span>*Figure 3.

--- SENTENCE 145 ---
Site-level density (no. colonies m<sup>−</sup>2) of adult (≥5 cm) coral colonies by site for reporting units in American Samoa surveyed in 2018.

--- SENTENCE 146 ---
*

Coral colony densities are mainly driven by the site-specific coral species assemblage and colony size distribution, together with competition for space.

--- SENTENCE 147 ---
In coral community assemblages, higher colony densities are generally indicative of a community skewed toward smaller colony sizes.

--- SENTENCE 148 ---
In 2018, colony densities were spatially variable with no clear trends for any reporting unit

--- SENTENCE 149 ---
The limited sample sizes attained during the 2018 coral demographic surveys at Swains Island, Ta'u Sanctuary, and Fagatele/Fagalua preclude any broad statements regarding trends and patterns in those Sanctuary management areas.

!

--- SENTENCE 150 ---
Site-level percent colony old mortality for reporting units in American Samoa surveyed in 2018.

--- SENTENCE 151 ---
*

Site-specific patterns of colony old mortality indicate that Rose Atoll reported the lowest levels compared to all other reporting units

--- SENTENCE 152 ---
This also suggests that corals at Rose Atoll probably sustained the lowest levels of colony mortality in the aftermath of the 2015 bleaching event; likely driven by reduced thermal stress during the 2015 bleaching event, as well as the successive warming events that occurred in 2016 and 2017

--- SENTENCE 153 ---
[](_page_13_Figure_1.jpeg)

<span id="page-13-0"></span>*Figure 5.

--- SENTENCE 154 ---
Site-level coral generic richness for reporting units in American Samoa surveyed in 2018.

--- SENTENCE 155 ---
*

Patterns of generic richness were consistent with historical observations, with higher number of genera per site observed Ofu and Olosega and Ta'ū compared to Tutuila (Brainard et al. 2008).

--- SENTENCE 156 ---
Generic richness at Fagatele Bay was unexpectedly low and may be biased by the limited survey effort (4 sites) and the selection of random sites that may have fallen on habitats that had low coral richness.

--- SENTENCE 157 ---
As expected, Swains Island and Rose Atoll exhibited lower levels of generic richness [\(Figure 5\)](#page-13-0) compared to the other reporting units.

!

--- SENTENCE 158 ---
[](_page_14_Figure_1.jpeg)

<span id="page-14-0"></span>*Figure 6.

--- SENTENCE 159 ---
Site-level occurrence of the coralline lethal orange disease (CLOD) by site for reporting units in American Samoa surveyed in 2018.

--- SENTENCE 160 ---
*

No lesions of CLOD were recorded at Rose Atoll and Swains Island.

--- SENTENCE 161 ---
Conspicuously high occurrence levels were found in the Fagatele/Fagalua and Aunu'u Sanctuary management areas [\(Figure 6\)](#page-14-0).

--- SENTENCE 162 ---
#### <span id="page-15-0"></span>2018 Stratum-level Estimates

The figures below illustrate mean depth stratum-level estimates of benthic cover

--- SENTENCE 163 ---
[\(Figure 7\)](#page-15-1); coral colony density (no. colonies m<sup>−</sup><sup>2</sup> ) and coral generic richness [\(Figure 8\)](#page-16-0); and colony old mortality and condition (prevalence of bleaching and diseases)

--- SENTENCE 164 ---
For these comparative spatial bar graphs, we pooled all site-level data within each depth stratum, within each of the reporting units listed in Table 1.

!

--- SENTENCE 165 ---
[](_page_15_Figure_3.jpeg)

<span id="page-15-1"></span>*Figure 7. Mean (± 1 SE) percent benthic cover of hard corals, crustose coralline algae (CCA), turf algae, and macroalgae by stratum (S = shallow: 0–6 m; M = mid-depth: >6–18 m, and D = deep: >18–30 m) for reporting units in American Samoa surveyed in 2018.

--- SENTENCE 166 ---
Absence of data for some reporting units indicates lack of surveys within that particular depth stratum.

--- SENTENCE 167 ---
Note the varying scale of the y-axes among plots.

--- SENTENCE 168 ---
*

Depth stratum level mean estimates for each reporting unit indicate, as expected, that coral cover was usually greater in the shallow and mid-depth strata compared to the deep habitats.

--- SENTENCE 169 ---
Rose Atoll and Tutuila SW were the only reporting units to exhibit greater coral cover at depth.

--- SENTENCE 170 ---
CCA cover was also greater on the shallow and mid-depth habitats, and in agreement with historical records, conspicuously high at Rose Atoll.

--- SENTENCE 171 ---
Turf algae cover varied between reporting units, as well as between depth strata; and fleshy macroalgae cover was consistently low between reporting unit strata with the exception of Swains Island.

--- SENTENCE 172 ---
There was a two- to three-fold difference in macroalgae cover on the mid-depth and deep strata at Swains compared to other reporting units [\(Figure 7\)](#page-15-1).

--- SENTENCE 173 ---
Density was greater for adult colonies than juvenile colonies for all reporting units and depth strata [\(Figure 8A\)](#page-16-0).

--- SENTENCE 174 ---
This trend is typical across most coral reefs systems in the U.S. Pacific Jurisdictions.

--- SENTENCE 175 ---
Generic richness [\(Figure 8B\)](#page-16-0) matched historical surveys.

--- SENTENCE 176 ---
While Ofu and Olosega harbored a greater number of genera per reporting unit than Tutuila (15–20 and 8–15, genera, respectively), overall islandwide generic richness was greater in Tutuila (29 genera) than Ofu and Olosega (22 genera) in 2018.

!

--- SENTENCE 177 ---
Mean (± 1 SE) estimates of: (A) density (colonies m<sup>−</sup><sup>2</sup>) of adult (A: ≥ 5 cm) and juvenile (J: <5 cm) coral colonies; (B) coral generic richness, by strata (shallow: 0–6 m; mid-depth: >6–18 m, and deep: >18–30 m) for reporting units in American Samoa surveyed in 2018.

--- SENTENCE 178 ---
Absence of data for some reporting units indicates lack of surveys within that particular depth stratum.*

--- SENTENCE 179 ---
With the exception of Ta'u, estimates of recent partial mortality were low across all reporting units, and old mortality was predictably higher in the shallow and mid-depth strata compared to the deep habitats

--- SENTENCE 180 ---
Differential thermal bleaching prevalence across depth strata [\(Figure 9B\)](#page-17-1) is generally the main driver determining the patterns of post-bleaching mortality.

--- SENTENCE 181 ---
Despite the spatial differences between depth strata and reporting units, the prevalence of acute and chronic diseases remained low after the 2015 bleaching event [\(Figure 9B\)](#page-17-1).

--- SENTENCE 182 ---
This is noteworthy, given that coral susceptibility to disease can be intensified by thermal stress events (Bruckner and Hill 2006).

!

--- SENTENCE 183 ---
[](_page_17_Figure_0.jpeg)

<span id="page-17-1"></span>*Figure 9.

--- SENTENCE 184 ---
Mean (± 1 SE) estimates of: (A) percent partial colony mortality (recent and old); and (B) prevalence (%) of bleaching and diseases (acute and chronic) by strata (S = shallow: 0–6 m; M = mid-depth: >6–18 m, and D = deep: >18–30 m) for reporting units in American Samoa surveyed in 2018.

--- SENTENCE 185 ---
Absence of data for some reporting units indicates lack of surveys within that particular depth stratum.

--- SENTENCE 186 ---
*

#### <span id="page-17-0"></span>Temporal Comparisons

--- SENTENCE 187 ---
The figures below illustrate mean estimates of benthic cover [\(Figure 10\)](#page-18-0), coral colony density (no. colonies m<sup>−</sup><sup>2</sup> and coral generic richness [\(Figure 11\)](#page-20-0), colony old mortality and condition (prevalence of bleaching and diseases)

--- SENTENCE 188 ---
[\(Figure 12\)](#page-22-0), and CLOD occurrence (Figure 13) contrasted between survey years 2015 and 2018 for those reporting units with appropriate samples sizes.

--- SENTENCE 189 ---
For these comparative spatial graphs (except CLOD occurrence), we pool all site-level data within each depth stratum, within each of the reporting units listed in Table 1.

--- SENTENCE 190 ---
Statistical temporal comparisons were made by assessing the 95% confidence interval of the difference between survey year means (Tables [4,](#page-19-0)

--- SENTENCE 191 ---
Despite a much limited survey effort in 2018 (due to weather conditions) compared to 2015, some general spatial patterns are described below.

--- SENTENCE 192 ---
For all reporting units combined, coral cover dropped from 28.7% ± 2.6 in 2015 to 18.2% ± 2.0 in 2018, representing a 36% decline.

--- SENTENCE 193 ---
CCA cover increased from 17.0 ± 1.6 in 2015 to 22.2 ± 2.5 in 2018 (30.1% increase), and macroalgae decreased from 3.4% ± 0.6 in 2015 to 0.7% ± 0.3 in 2018.

--- SENTENCE 194 ---
Significant mean coral cover losses between 2015 and 2018 were reported for Rose Atoll Sanctuary (mean ± SE: 20.1 ± 1.7 and 14.9 ± 1.3, respectively), Ofu and Olosega (32.4% ± 1.6 and 20.8% ± 1.4, respectively), Ta'u non-Sanctuary (33.5% ± 2 and 25.9% ± 2.6, respectively), NE Tutuila (31.5% ± 3.3 and 18.2% ± 2.8, respectively), and NW Tutuila (24.6% ± 3.9 and 14.5% ±1.7, respectively)

--- SENTENCE 195 ---
It is important to note that the temporal coral cover loss reported for Fagatele/Fagalua is likely biased by the limited survey effort attained in 2018 (16 sites) compared to 2015 (48 surveys)

--- SENTENCE 196 ---
Local experts agree that losses within this Sanctuary management area were not as considerable as illustrated in Figure 10 (Sudek and Vargas-Ángel, pers.

--- SENTENCE 197 ---
Likewise, benthic cover changes reported for SE Tutuila need to be interpreted with caution given the limited number of sites surveyed in 2018 compared to 2015 (7 and 68 sites, respectively.

--- SENTENCE 198 ---
A significant increase in CCA cover was found at Rose Atoll Sanctuary management area, Ta'u non-Sanctuary, and NE and NW Tutuila

--- SENTENCE 199 ---
Rose Atoll and Swains Island exhibited the greatest levels of CCA both before and after the 2015 bleaching event [\(Figure 10\)](#page-18-0).

--- SENTENCE 200 ---
The 2018 levels of CCA at Swains were historically high (mean ± SE: 44.6 ± 4.6).

--- SENTENCE 201 ---
These values are close to those recorded at Rose Atoll, which are the highest among the ~40 island and atolls periodically visited by NOAA's Pacific RAMP.

--- SENTENCE 202 ---
The decrease in coral cover and increase in CCA at Swains Island likely emerged from the mass bleaching event that resulted in the mortality of *Pocillopora* corals.

--- SENTENCE 203 ---
Coral cover declined more than 70%, from 15.3% ± 3.5 in 2015 to 4.2% ± 1.6 in 2018 (mean ± SE), creating open space for the proliferation of CCA over the dead coral skeletons.

!

--- SENTENCE 204 ---
Temporal comparison (2015 and 2018) of mean (± 1 SE) percent benthic cover of hard corals, crustose coralline algae (CCA), macroalgae, and turf algae for reporting units in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 205 ---
Note the varying ranges of y-axes among plots.

--- SENTENCE 206 ---
*

Turf algae cover was spatially variable both before and after the 2015 coral bleaching.

--- SENTENCE 207 ---
Significant losses of turf were reported for NE Tutuila

--- SENTENCE 208 ---
Overall, Rose Atoll exhibited the lowest levels of turf algae both before and after the bleaching event.

--- SENTENCE 230 ---
Although not illustrated in Figure 10, levels of the encrusting macroalgae, *Peyssonnelia*, registered a nearly 200% increase for all reporting units after the 2015 bleaching event.

--- SENTENCE 231 ---
Increased levels of encrusting macroalgae following a major bleaching event were also reported at other reefs in the Pacific Remote Islands Marine National Monument following the 2015– 2016 bleaching event, including Baker, Howland, and Jarvis islands, as well as Wake Atoll (Vargas-Ángel et al. 2019).

!

--- SENTENCE 232 ---
[](_page_20_Figure_1.jpeg)

<span id="page-20-0"></span>*Figure 11.

--- SENTENCE 233 ---
Temporal comparison (2015 and 2018) of mean (± 1 SE): (A) density (colonies m<sup>−</sup>2) of adult (≥ 5 cm) and juvenile (<5 cm) of coral colonies; and (B) generic coral richness for reporting units in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 234 ---
Although adult colony densities varied between survey years

--- SENTENCE 235 ---
[\(Figure 11A\)](#page-20-0), differences were not significant, except for NE Tutuila

--- SENTENCE 236 ---
[\(Table 5\)](#page-21-0) where numbers increased in 2018.

--- SENTENCE 237 ---
No statistical temporal comparisons were conducted for Fagatele/Fagalua or Swains due to the unbalanced sample sizes between survey years

--- SENTENCE 238 ---
Local experts agree that colony density increases within the Fagatele/Fagalua Sanctuary management area were not as considerable as illustrated in Figure 11A (Sudek and Vargas-Ángel pers.

--- SENTENCE 239 ---
At Swains Island, adult coral colony density losses were driven by a staggering >60% loss of *Pocillopora* colonies.

--- SENTENCE 240 ---
In contrast to the Fagatele/Fagalua Sanctuary management, local experts (Sudek and Lawrence pers.

--- SENTENCE 241 ---
obs.) agree that the 2015 bleaching event had a visible effect in the coral community demographics at Swains Island.

--- SENTENCE 242 ---
Juvenile colonies (<5 cm diameter) also varied between 2015 and 2018

--- SENTENCE 243 ---
[\(Figure 11A\)](#page-20-0), but differences were not significant except for NE Tutuila

--- SENTENCE 244 ---
This increase may have been driven by a post-bleaching recruitment pulse over newly available substrate.

--- SENTENCE 245 ---
Generic richness remained unchanged between survey years

--- SENTENCE 246 ---
Significant betweenyear differences for NE Tutuila were driven by the lack of 2018 surveys in the shallow depth stratum

--- SENTENCE 247 ---
[\(Figure 8\)](#page-16-0): mean generic richness for the mid-depth and deep strata combined remained unchanged between survey years (mean ± SE; 8.4 ± 1.2 in 2015 and 8.8 ± 0.5 in 2018).

--- SENTENCE 248 ---
Old mortality decreased for most reporting units, except at NE Tutuila and Swains

--- SENTENCE 249 ---
Fission of colonies following partial mortality, together with the associated challenges to determine colony boundaries, particularly for encrusting corals abundantly present in Tutuila and Manu'a, may be

implicated in the lower post-bleaching levels of partial mortality.

--- SENTENCE 250 ---
In addition, while the higher levels of coral bleaching reported in the 2015 surveys [\(Figure 12B\)](#page-22-0) are expected based on the timing of the event and the monitoring survey, background levels of bleaching (<5%) in 2018 for Rose Atoll and Swains Island likely represent natural colony pallor common on these reef systems.

--- SENTENCE 251 ---
Acute and chronic coral diseases remained at background levels (1–2%) with no significant differences between years

#### <span id="page-29-0"></span>Temporal Comparisons

The following section illustrates mean reporting unit-level estimates of biomass density (g m<sup>−</sup><sup>2</sup> ) of all fishes, the striped surgeonfish, *Acanthurus lineatus*, piscivores, and fishes >50 cm TL

--- SENTENCE 349 ---
[\(Figure 20\)](#page-30-0); and reef fish species and generic richness

--- SENTENCE 350 ---
[\(Figure 21\)](#page-31-0) across survey years (2015, 2016, and 2018).

--- SENTENCE 351 ---
Statistical temporal comparisons (based on the 95% confidence interval of the difference between survey year means; i.e., 2015 and 2018) for each reporting unit are presented in Table 7 and Table 8.

--- SENTENCE 352 ---
Despite a highly limited survey effort in 2018 (due to weather conditions) compared to 2015, some general spatial patterns are described below.

--- SENTENCE 353 ---
Biomass of all fishes surveyed significantly increased between 2015 and 2018 at Fagatele and Rose Sanctuary management areas, as well as Ta'u, and SE Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 354 ---
The remaining reporting units did not significantly differ in fish biomass between those survey years [\(Figure 20A](#page-30-0) and [Table 7\)](#page-31-1).

--- SENTENCE 355 ---
Substantial increases in the biomass of *Acanthurus lineatus* between 2015 and 2018 were recorded at NW Tutuila, SE Tutuila, and Ofu and Olosega; however, observed biomass was also highly variable at those reporting units [\(Figure 20B\)](#page-30-0).

--- SENTENCE 356 ---
Reporting units in which large and piscivorous fishes were observed in relatively high levels of mean biomass also had large variance

--- SENTENCE 357 ---
[\(Figure 20C, D\)](#page-30-0), likely due to the patchiness of occurrence of such species that may also be intermittently observed in large schools.

!

--- SENTENCE 358 ---
[](_page_30_Figure_0.jpeg)

<span id="page-30-0"></span>*Figure 20.

--- SENTENCE 359 ---
Biomass density (g m-2) of: (A) all fishes; (B) striped surgeonfish,* **Acanthurus lineatus***; (C) piscivorous fishes; (D) and fishes >50 cm TL for reporting units in American Samoa surveyed in 2015, 2016, and 2018.

--- SENTENCE 360 ---
Note the varying ranges of y-axes among plots.

--- SENTENCE 361 ---
*

Species and genus richness of fishes appeared to increase between 2015 and 2018 in all surveyed reporting units [\(Figure 21\)](#page-31-0), and indeed, species richness significantly increased over three years in all reporting units except for Swains Sanctuary [\(Table 8\)](#page-32-3).

--- SENTENCE 362 ---
Importantly, these increasing trends are very likely driven by sampling biases among teams of divers between 2015 and 2018, especially since this increasing trend was consistent across all reporting units.

--- SENTENCE 363 ---
Therefore, it is unclear in which instances increases in species and genus richness reflect a true broadening in diversity of fish communities.

!

## <span id="page-32-0"></span>**2018 Ocean and Climate Change**

#### <span id="page-32-1"></span>2018 Site-level Data

The following section displays site-level bubble maps, which illustrate the spatial distribution and depth of subsurface temperature recorders, Calcification Accretion Units, and dissolved inorganic carbon (DIC) water samples to estimate Omega aragonite.

--- SENTENCE 384 ---
A concatenated time series of in-situ water temperature is also included.

--- SENTENCE 385 ---
A summary table displaying site-level DIC water chemistry data is presented in [Appendix](#page-53-0)

--- SENTENCE 386 ---
[5.](#page-53-0)

A total of 37 temperature loggers were recovered [\(Figure 22\)](#page-33-1), 85 water samples for carbonate chemistry collected [\(Figure 23\)](#page-34-0), and 102 CAU units recovered [\(Figure 24\)](#page-35-0) between June 28 and August 16, 2018.

#### <span id="page-32-2"></span>Sea Surface Temperature

The timing and magnitude of temperature patterns in the Territory are similar across islands and Sanctuary management areas

--- SENTENCE 387 ---
A strong seasonal cycle is evident, with the warmest temperatures occurring during summer months (Dec–Mar) and cooler temperatures observed in the winter (July–September).

--- SENTENCE 388 ---
From the period of temperature data collection, 2010 through 2015, temperatures across all islands rarely exceeded the bleaching threshold —i.e., 1°C above the maximum monthly mean; and if exceeded, it was over a very narrow time period.

--- SENTENCE 389 ---
In 2015–2017, however, summer temperatures exceeded the bleaching threshold for several months during each year.

--- SENTENCE 390 ---
These sharp and repeated ocean temperature rises during the 2015–2016 El Niño caused prolonged thermal stress and subsequent coral bleaching, which was recorded by the coral demographic surveys in March–April 2015.

--- SENTENCE 391 ---
During this warming event, Swains Island experienced the greatest thermal anomalies, with water temperatures of almost 31°C and consequently, widespread levels of coral bleaching (NOAA 2016).

--- SENTENCE 392 ---
By the summer of 2018, the ocean warming event had receded and temperatures were mild, well within the typical range.

--- SENTENCE 393 ---
### <span id="page-33-0"></span>Carbonate Chemistry

Omega aragonite values in American Samoa are traditionally the highest measured among the 48 islands and atolls periodically visited by NOAA's Pacific RAMP.

--- SENTENCE 394 ---
Within the islands of American Samoa there is little temporal variation; omega aragonite values were very similar between survey years 2015 and 2018.

--- SENTENCE 395 ---
Rose Atoll exhibited the highest omega aragonite levels while Tutuila had the lowest [\(Figure 23\)](#page-34-0).

--- SENTENCE 396 ---
Fagatele and Fagalua Sanctuary management areas have lower omega aragonite values than most of the forereef sampling locations.

--- SENTENCE 397 ---
In small, sheltered embayments like Fagatele and Fagalua, with limited water circulation and longer residence times, biological activity (e.g., calcification and respiration) can draw omega aragonite.

!

--- SENTENCE 398 ---
Spatial distribution and depth of subsurface temperature loggers maintained in American Samoa.
